Stability of Rayleigh–Jeans Equilibria in the Kinetic FPU Equation
Authors: Pierre Germain, Joonhyun La, Angeliki Menegaki
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We study the nonlinear dynamics of the kinetic wave equation associated with the FPU problem and prove the stability of the non-singular Rayleigh–Jeans equilibria. The lack of a spectral gap for the linearized problem leads to polynomial decay, which we are able to leverage to obtain nonlinear stability.

Introduction

The microscopic model

In their groundbreaking 1955 study [7], Fermi, Pasta, Ulam and Tsingou utilized the early electronic computers to explore the relaxation dynamics (thermalisation) of a chain of coupled nonlinear oscillators. The Fermi-Pasta-Ulam-Tsingou system is an anharmonic chain of oscillators without pinning potential. The system is described by its momentum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(p_n)_{n \in \mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}(pn)n∈Z and position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(q_n)_{n \in \mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}(qn)n∈Z with the Hamiltonian energy given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(p,q) = \sum _{n \in \mathbb {Z}} \frac{p_n^2}{2} + \sum _{n \in \mathbb {Z}} F(q_{n+1} - q_n), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(p,q)=∑n∈Zpn22+∑n∈ZF(qn+1-qn),where F is smooth and satisfies F(0) = 0F(0)=0.

The dynamics are governed by the system of ODEs\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ -\ddot{q_n} = F''(q_{n+1}-q_n) - F''(q_n - q_{n-1}). $$\end{document}-qn¨=F′′(qn+1-qn)-F′′(qn-qn-1).A particular example is the so-called FPUT-β β case\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ F(x) = \frac{1}{2} x^2 + \frac{\beta }{4} x^4, \text {with } \beta >0, $$\end{document}F(x)=12x2+β4x4,withβ>0,which will be the focus of the present article.

Motivated by the original FPUT problem and its substantial impact [4], significant attention has been given to the energy transport within oscillator chains of length N coupled to thermal reservoirs at different temperatures. Numerically, it is observed that in β β-FPUT chains, the thermal conductivity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _N$$\end{document}κN diverges as N increases in an anomalous way, specifically as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa _N \sim N^{2/5}$$\end{document}κN∼N2/5 [1, 9]. An alternative approach is to consider an infinite chain and observe energy spread after injecting energy at the origin. This reformulates the problem to that of studying the time-decay of the energy current-current correlation function, which is related to the decay rate of the linearized semigroup of the wave turbulence kinetic equation arising from such microscopic models [2]. For the linearised problem of the β β-FPUT chain, it was proven in [11] that the correlation decays as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(t^{-3/5})$$\end{document}O(t-3/5), aligning with numerical findings. Following the study in [11], in [12] it was rigorously derived a macroscopic fractional diffusion equation describing heat transport in β β-FPUT chains, confirming the anomalous diffusion behavior from the linearized Boltzmann phonon equation. Our main inspiration and motivation here is also the findings in [11].

We also mention other classical choices of nonlinear atom chains. Beside the β β-FPU chain, one may consider the α α-FPU chain, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(x) = \frac{x^2}{2} + \alpha \frac{x^3}{3}$$\end{document}F(x)=x22+αx33, or the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha +\beta $$\end{document}α+β-FPU chain, which combines the nonlinearities of both the α α and β β chains. Moreover, adding an additional pinning potential to the dynamics results in the Discrete Nonlinear Klein-Gordon chain, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{KG}(x) = \frac{\delta }{2}x^2$$\end{document}FKG(x)=δ2x2 and the energy includes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_{pin}(q) = (\frac{1}{2}-\delta ) q^2 + \frac{1}{4} q^4$$\end{document}Upin(q)=(12-δ)q2+14q4. Finally, the Toda lattice is yet another model, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_T(x) = \frac{1}{4\alpha ^2}(e^{2\alpha x}-1-2\alpha x)$$\end{document}FT(x)=14α2(e2αx-1-2αx). For a detailed account and discussion on these models, we refer to the reviews [10, 13, 14].

The homogeneous kinetic wave equation

In this article, we focus on the kinetic wave equation, or phonon Boltzmann equation, arising from the β β-FPUT system. Finally, the Rayleigh–Jeans equilibria (RJ)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathfrak {f}_{\beta ,\gamma }(p) = \frac{1}{\beta \omega (p) + \gamma }, \qquad \beta ,\gamma \ge 0 $$\end{document}fβ,γ(p)=1βω(p)+γ,β,γ≥0are the unique stationary solutions [11, Section 5].

The case γ =0γ=0 corresponds to a singular RJ equilibrium, which has infinite mass. However, it is favored by some authors since it gives equipartition of energy, as was initially expected by E. Fermi et.al. in their numerical experiment [10, 13].

Interestingly, not all couples \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M}_0,\mathcal {E}_0)$$\end{document}(M0,E0) can be associated to a RJ equilibrium, if the inverse temperature is positive, i.e. β >0β>0. For initial data whose mass and energy cannot be matched to a RJ equilibrium, the asymptotic behavior of the solution is an intriguing question - see the discussion in Sect. 7 and [6], where we provide a characterization of the entropy maximizers. Allowing also for negative temperatures, we prove that we are always (for any pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M}_0,\mathcal {E}_0)$$\end{document}(M0,E0)) able to find RJ equilibria having the associated mass and energy.

Making sense of the collision operator

The expression for the collision operator in (1.1) is not obviously meaningful, even for smooth functions f. Indeed, it involves the product of two δ δ functions, which, as is well-known, can be ill-defined.

As was shown in [11] (see also Lemma 12), the common zeros of Ω Ω and Σ Σ are of two kinds. On the one hand, the trivial zeros are such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ p_0, p_1 \} = \{ p_2, p_3 \}$$\end{document}{p0,p1}={p2,p3}. The integrand of the collision operator vanishes identically on the set of trivial zeros, which provides some cancellation. However, from the viewpoint of distribution theory, the formal collision kernel involves the singular product \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta (\Omega ) \delta (\Sigma )$$\end{document}δ(Ω)δ(Σ), and so it is not clear how to make sense of the product of two Dirac δ δ, even if it is evaluated on a function vanishing on their singular set ("indefinite form"). In the linearized setting, Lukkarinen and Spohn in [11] justify the cancellation by by introducing a regularization of the singular resonance constraint and then passing to the limit. In that limit, only the nontrivial branch, cf. (1.2), survives in the final expression for the operator. In that sense, the trivial zeros \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ p_0, p_1 \} = \{ p_2, p_3 \}$$\end{document}{p0,p1}={p2,p3} do not contribute. This regularization is closely related to the microscopic derivation of the equation (which remains an outstanding open problem) and we shall take for granted that trivial zeros can be ignored in the collision operator.

On the other hand, non-trivial zeros can be parameterized by1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_1 = h(p_0,p_2) \mod 2\pi , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p1=h(p0,p2)mod2π,where1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} h(x,z) = \frac{z-x}{2} + 2 \arcsin \left( \tan \frac{|z-x|}{4} \cos \frac{z+x}{4} \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h(x,z)=z-x2+2arcsintan|z-x|4cosz+x4.Using this expression, a calculation ([11] or Lemma 13) shows that the collision operator can be written as1.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}[f](p_0)= \int _0^{2\pi } \frac{ \omega _0 \omega _1 \omega _2 \omega _3 }{\sqrt{F_+(p_0,p_2)}} \prod _{\ell =0}^3 f_\ell \left( \frac{1}{f}+\frac{1}{f_1}-\frac{1}{f_2}-\frac{1}{f_3}\right) dp_2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C[f](p0)=∫02πω0ω1ω2ω3F+(p0,p2)∏ℓ=03fℓ1f+1f1-1f2-1f3dp2,where it is understood that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} & p_3 = p_0 + p_1 - p_2\\ & p_1 = h(p_0,p_2). \end{array}\right. } $$\end{document}p3=p0+p1-p2p1=h(p0,p2).and1.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_{\pm }(p_0,p_2) = \left[ \cos \left( \frac{p_0}{2}\right) +\cos \left( \frac{p_2}{2}\right) \right] ^2 \pm 4\sin \left( \frac{p_0}{2}\right) \sin \left( \frac{p_2}{2}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F±(p0,p2)=cosp02+cosp222±4sinp02sinp22.

Main results and organization of the paper

Section 3 establishes basic local well-posedness results in weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty $$\end{document}L∞ spaces. It also shows that local well-posedness cannot be expected (or at least is very delicate) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^p$$\end{document}Lp-based spaces for p < 3p<3. The results of this section (Theorems 1 and 2) can be summarized as the following theorem:

Theorem

The collision operator is bounded in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^\alpha L^\infty $$\end{document}ωαL∞ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > -\frac{5}{4}$$\end{document}α>-54. Thus, the equation is locally well-posed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C} (\omega ^\alpha L^\infty )$$\end{document}C(ωαL∞) (the space of continuous functions with values in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^\alpha L^\infty $$\end{document}ωαL∞ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > -\frac{5}{4}$$\end{document}α>-54).

The collision operator is unbounded on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^p$$\end{document}Lp for any p < 3p<3.

In Section 4, we turn to the linearized problem around RJ equilibria, recapitulating many results obtained in [11], and providing some extensions.

Theorem

We denote by L the linearized operator around a RJ solution with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ,\gamma >0$$\end{document}β,γ>0. This estimate is insufficient for nonlinear purposes: the decay in time is weak, and we cannot hope to close nonlinear estimates since the equation is ill-posed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 type spaces. Furthermore, the lack of a spectral gap leads to the degenerate weight a(p).

In Section 5 we address these shortcomings by proving pointwise decay at a polynomial rate (Theorem 3).

Theorem

Let L be the linearized operator around a RJ solution with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ,\gamma >0$$\end{document}β,γ>0. Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_0 \in L^\infty $$\end{document}g0∈L∞, with a zero projection (in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Ker} L$$\end{document}KerL, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi g_0=0$$\end{document}Πg0=0. Then, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ,\nu \in [\frac{1}{6}, \frac{1}{2}]$$\end{document}μ,ν∈[16,12] and any δ >0δ>0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert \omega ^\mu e^{tL} g_0 \Vert _{L^\infty } \lesssim _\delta \langle t \rangle ^{- \frac{3}{5}(\mu + \nu )+ \delta } \Vert \omega ^{-\nu } g_0 \Vert _{L^\infty }. $$\end{document}‖ωμetLg0‖L∞≲δ⟨t⟩-35(μ+ν)+δ‖ω-νg0‖L∞.

This is achieved by understanding how the edges of the frequency domain, where dissipation degenerates, interact with the bulk of the domain. At a more technical level, we resort to an iterative scheme to gain decay increasingly.

Finally, Section 6 deals with the fully nonlinear problem, showing global existence of solutions around non-singular RJ. This is accomplished through a careful definition of an appropriate norm that allows us to control the nonlinearity, relying crucially on the structure of the equation (Theorem 4).

Theorem

There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _0$$\end{document}ϵ0 such that the following holds: if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ f(t=0) = \mathfrak {f}_{\beta ,\gamma } [ 1 + g_0], $$\end{document}f(t=0)=fβ,γ[1+g0],where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int \mathfrak {f}_{\beta ,\gamma } g_0 \, \textrm{d}p = \int \omega \mathfrak {f}_{\beta ,\gamma } g_0 \, \textrm{d}p = 0$$\end{document}∫fβ,γg0dp=∫ωfβ,γg0dp=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \omega ^{-\frac{1}{2}} g_0 \Vert _{L^\infty } = \epsilon < \epsilon _0$$\end{document}‖ω-12g0‖L∞=ϵ<ϵ0, then there exists a global solution which can be written as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ f(t) = \mathfrak {f}_{\beta ,\gamma } [ 1 + g] \qquad \text{ where } \quad \Vert \omega ^{\frac{1}{2}} g(t,p) \Vert _{L^\infty _p} \lesssim \epsilon \langle t \rangle ^{-\frac{3}{5} + \frac{1}{1000}} \quad \text{ for } \text{ all } t \ge 0. $$\end{document}f(t)=fβ,γ[1+g]where‖ω12g(t,p)‖Lp∞≲ϵ⟨t⟩-35+11000forallt≥0.

The appendix gathers some elementary but involved computations which are used in the rest of the text.

Stability of equilibria for the Boltzmann equation

It is worthwhile comparing the results which have been stated above to analogous statements for the spatially homogeneous Boltzmann equation. In the case of the classical Boltzmann equation, the nonlinearities are quadratic, unlike the cubic nonlinearities of the wave turbulence equation, and the equilibrium is the Maxwellian distribution. Depending on the collision kernel, it is known that the linearized operator around the equilibrium possesses a spectral gap (for instance in the hard sphere case), leading to exponential convergence to equilibrium. However, in the soft potential case, there is no spectral gap, and the decay of the linearized operator is at most of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-t^{\sigma }}$$\end{document}e-tσ, σ <1σ<1. For more details, see the reviews and articles [3, 8, 16] and references therein.

Roughly speaking, the slightly weaker decay under soft potentials can be attributed to the fact that the collision frequency in the kernel of the Boltzmann operator is not lower-bounded and degenerates at large velocities. However, since the perturbative regime is around a Maxwellian distribution, the initial data roughly resembles a Maxwellian, making the large velocity regime, where the spectral gap becomes zero, less significant.

In our case, we also encounter a degenerate spectral gap that becomes zero at the edges of the frequency domain. This type of degeneracy is very different in nature and significantly affects the decay of the linearized semigroup.

Finally, the spatially inhomogeneous Boltzmann equation has been extensively studied, presenting additional mathematical challenges related to the existence of local equilibria. The theory of hypocoercivity has been developed for such inhomogeneous kinetic models, see [5, 15, 17] and references therein.

Local well-posedness

Local well-posedness in weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty $$\end{document}L∞ spaces

Theorem 1

This Cauchy problem is locally well-posed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^{\alpha } L^\infty (\mathbb {T})$$\end{document}ωαL∞(T) if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > - \frac{5}{4}$$\end{document}α>-54. Remark 1

What is the significance of the above theorem? The simplest case is α =0α=0, which simply gives local well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty $$\end{document}L∞. The case α =-1α=-1 is important since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^{-1} L^\infty $$\end{document}ω-1L∞ is a function space which includes the singular Rayleigh–Jeans equilibria \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {f}_{\beta ,0}$$\end{document}fβ,0. Finally, the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ne 0$$\end{document}α≠0 can be interpreted as the propagation by the nonlinear problem of the vanishing or singular behavior at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=0 \mod 2\pi $$\end{document}p=0mod2π - related ideas will play an important role in the nonlinear stability questions examined later in this paper. Finally from the last part of the proof, we expect the exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-\frac{5}{4}$$\end{document}-54 to be the sharp one.

In fact, using the explicit parametrisation of the resonant manifold, (1.2), which we recall is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_1= & h(p_0,p_2),\quad p_3 = p_0+h-p_2, \quad h(p_0,p_2) = \frac{p_2-p_0}{2}\\ & \quad + 2 \arcsin \left( \tan \frac{|p_2-p_0|}{4} \cos \frac{p_2+p_0}{4} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p1=h(p0,p2),p3=p0+h-p2,h(p0,p2)=p2-p02+2arcsintan|p2-p0|4cosp2+p04,we can sharpen the threshold for α α. Namely, we will show that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ Q_\alpha (p_0,p_2)\!:=\! \sqrt{\omega _0 \omega _2} \omega _1 \omega _3 \prod _{\ell =1}^3 \omega _\ell ^{\alpha } \left( {\omega _0^{-\alpha }}\!+\! {\omega _1^{-\alpha }}\!+\!{\omega _2^{-\alpha }}\!+\!{\omega _3^{-\alpha }}\right) \!\lesssim \! \max (1,\omega _2^{\frac{3}{2} + 2\alpha }).$$\end{document}Qα(p0,p2):=ω0ω2ω1ω3∏ℓ=13ωℓαω0-α+ω1-α+ω2-α+ω3-α≲max(1,ω232+2α).We focus on the region where both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_0, p_2$$\end{document}p0,p2 are close to 0. In particular, due to symmetry, we treat only the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<p_2<p_0 \ll 1$$\end{document}0<p2<p0≪1. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_0,p_2$$\end{document}p0,p2 are far from the edges, or when one of them is, the same (or simpler) bounds hold.

Note that these calculations indicate that the threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > -\frac{5}{4}$$\end{document}α>-54, is in fact the optimal one for the local well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^{\alpha } L^\infty (\mathbb {T})$$\end{document}ωαL∞(T). □ □

Unboundedness in weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^p$$\end{document}Lp spaces for p<3p<3

In the preceding subsection, we saw that local well-posedness in weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty $$\end{document}L∞ spaces was a consequence of the boundedness of the collision operator in these spaces. In the present section, we show that the collision operator cannot be bounded on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^p$$\end{document}Lp-type spaces for p<3p<3. This does not imply ill-posedness, but this shows that well-posedness can only be the result of a very delicate nonlinear mechanism.

From now on we will write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \underline{h}(x,z) = x-z + h(x,z) = \frac{x-z}{2} + 2 \arcsin \left( \tan \frac{|z-x|}{4} \cos \frac{z+x}{4} \right) \mod 2\pi . $$\end{document}h_(x,z)=x-z+h(x,z)=x-z2+2arcsintan|z-x|4cosz+x4mod2π.

Theorem 2

The collision operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C is not bounded on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^p(\mathbb {T})$$\end{document}Lp(T) if p<3p<3.

Proof

Three specific points. Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0$$\end{document}x0, we choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \mapsto h(x_0,z)$$\end{document}z↦h(x0,z) reaches its global maximum at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0$$\end{document}z0 (this maximum is unique modulo 2π 2π for almost all values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0$$\end{document}x0). The numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p^0_0,p^0_1, p^0_2$$\end{document}p00,p10,p20 are distinct and different from 0 (modulo 2π 2π).

If the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(p_i)$$\end{document}(pi) belong to the resonant manifold, meaning that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_3 = p_0 + p_1 - The next four lemmas lay out the basic properties of the linear operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L,A,K_1,K_2$$\end{document}L,A,K1,K2. Lemma 1

(Kernel of the linearized operator) The kernel of L is spanned by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {f}$$\end{document}f and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \mathfrak {f}$$\end{document}ωf.

Proof

This follows immediately from the characterization of collisional invariants, Theorem 2.2 in [11]. □ □

Lemma 2

(Asymptotics of the multiplier function) For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ,\gamma >0$$\end{document}β,γ>0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ a(p) \sim _{\beta ,\gamma } \sin \left( \frac{p}{2} \right) ^{\frac{5}{3}}. $$\end{document}a(p)∼β,γsinp253.

Proof

This result is proven in Lemmas 16 and 18, for zero and non-zero mass, respectively. □ □

Lemma 3

(Compactness of the weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_2$$\end{document}K2 operator) For any β β, γ γ, the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-\frac{1}{2}} K_2 a^{-\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}a-12K2a-12 is compact on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2.

Proof

Using Lemma 2 and the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{F_+} \ge \sqrt{\omega _0 \omega _2}$$\end{document}F+≥ω0ω2, we find that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ a(p)^{-\frac{1}{2}} K_2(p,p_2) a(p_2)^{-\frac{1}{2}} \lesssim \sin \left( \frac{p}{2} \right) ^{-\frac{1}{3}} \sin \left( \frac{p_2}{2} \right) ^{- \frac{1}{3}}, $$\end{document}a(p)-12K2(p,p2)a(p2)-12≲sinp2-13sinp22-13,so that the corresponding operator is Hilbert-Schmidt, and therefore compact on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2. □ □

Lemma 4

(Compactness of the weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1$$\end{document}K1 operator) For any β β, γ γ, the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-\frac{1}{2}} K_1 a^{- \frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}a-12K1a-12 is compact on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{K}_1 = a^{-\frac{1}{2}} K_1 a^{- \frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}K~1=a-12K1a-12. \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert \widetilde{K}_1^{(1)} \Vert _{L^2 \rightarrow L^2} \lesssim \delta ^{\frac{1}{6}}, \qquad \Vert \widetilde{K}_1^{(2)} \Vert \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖K~1(3)(p,p1)‖Lp,p1∞:=esssup(p,p1)∈T2|K~1(3)(p,p1)|≲ϵ,δ1.These bounds imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} By closedness of the class of compact operators, this implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{K}_1$$\end{document}K~1 is compact.

There remains to prove the bounds on the operator norms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{K}_1^{(1)}$$\end{document}K~1(1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{K}_1^{(2)}$$\end{document}K~1(2). Combining these four lemmas, we will obtain a crucial lower bound on the linearized operator.

Proposition 4.1

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta ,\gamma >0$$\end{document}β,γ>0, we have for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in L^2$$\end{document}g∈L2 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \perp _{L^2_a} \operatorname {Ker} L$$\end{document}g⊥La2KerL,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle -L g,g\rangle \gtrsim \int a(p) |g(p)|^2 \, \textrm{d}p. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨-Lg,g⟩≳∫a(p)|g(p)|2dp.

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{K} = a^{-\frac{1}{2}} K a^{- \frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}K~=a-12Ka-12. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0 \le - \langle L g,g \rangle = \langle A g,g \rangle - \langle K g,g \rangle&= \langle a^{\frac{1}{2}} g, a^{\frac{1}{2}} g \rangle - \langle \widetilde{K} (a^{\frac{1}{2}} g), (a^{\frac{1}{2}} g) \rangle \\&= \langle (\operatorname {Id} - \widetilde{K}) a^{\frac{1}{2}} g ,a^{\frac{1}{2}} g \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0≤-⟨Lg,g⟩=⟨Ag,g⟩-⟨Kg,g⟩=⟨a12g,a12g⟩-⟨K~(a12g),(a12g)⟩=⟨(Id-K~)a12g,a12g⟩.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{K}$$\end{document}K~ is compact and self-adjoint, its spectrum is discrete away from 0. It follows from the above formula that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{K}$$\end{document}K~ cannot have eigenvalues >1>1, due to the positive sign of the Dirichlet form, and that the eigenspace associated to the eigenvalue 1 of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{K}$$\end{document}K~ coincides with the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{\frac{1}{2}}\operatorname {Ker} L$$\end{document}a12KerL, i.e. that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Ker} (I - \widetilde{K}) = a^{\frac{1}{2}}\operatorname {Ker} L$$\end{document}Ker(I-K~)=a12KerL. Indeed,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{K}f = f \iff a^{-\frac{1}{2}} K a^{-\frac{1}{2}} f=f \iff Kg=Ag \iff L g=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K~f=f⟺a-12Ka-12f=f⟺Kg=Ag⟺Lg=0,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f =a^{\frac{1}{2}} g$$\end{document}f=a12g, where we stress that L is acting on the original variable g, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{K}$$\end{document}K~ acts on the conjugated variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{\frac{1}{2}}g$$\end{document}a12g, in flat \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2.

Finally, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f =a^{\frac{1}{2}} g \in \operatorname {Ker}(\operatorname {Id} - \widetilde{K})^\perp $$\end{document}f=a12g∈Ker(Id-K~)⊥, then due to spectral theorem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle (\operatorname {Id} - \widetilde{K})f,f \rangle \ge \delta \Vert f \Vert _{L^2}^2$$\end{document}⟨(Id-K~)f,f⟩≥δ‖f‖L22 for some δ >0δ>0, and thus\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ - \langle L g,g \rangle = \langle A g,g \rangle - \langle \widetilde{K} a^{\frac{1}{2}} g, a^{\frac{1}{2}} g \rangle \gtrsim \int a(p) |g(p)|^2 \, \textrm{d}p. $$\end{document}-⟨Lg,g⟩=⟨Ag,g⟩-⟨K~a12g,a12g⟩≳∫a(p)|g(p)|2dp.□ □

Turning to the evolution problem, we note first that it admits a unique solution for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 data.

Lemma 5

(Existence and uniqueness) For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_0 \in L^2$$\end{document}g0∈L2, there exists a unique solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(t,p) \in \mathcal {C}([0,\infty ],L^2)$$\end{document}g(t,p)∈C([0,∞],L2) to the Cauchy problem\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \partial _t g - Lg = 0\\ g(t=0) = g_0 \end{array}\right. } $$\end{document}∂tg-Lg=0g(t=0)=g0which we will denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(t) = e^{tL} g_0$$\end{document}g(t)=etLg0. Furthermore, its \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm is bounded by that of the data:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \text{ for } \text{ any } t \ge 0, \qquad \Vert g(t) \Vert _{L^2} \le \Vert g_0 \Vert _{L^2}. $$\end{document}foranyt≥0,‖g(t)‖L2≤‖g0‖L2.

Proof

We saw that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-\frac{1}{2}} K_1 a^{-\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}a-12K1a-12 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-\frac{1}{2}} K_2 a^{-\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}a-12K2a-12 are bounded on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2, hence this is also the case for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1$$\end{document}K1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_2$$\end{document}K2. Therefore, L is bounded and the lemma follows by a fixed point theorem. That the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm is non-increasing follows from a simple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 energy estimate, since L is dissipative, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle Lg,g\rangle \le 0$$\end{document}⟨Lg,g⟩≤0. □ □

As a consequence of the lower bound proved in the previous proposition, we get the following corollary:

Corollary 1

(Dissipation inequality) For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_0 \in L^2 \cap \big ( \operatorname {Ker} L\big )^{\perp _a}$$\end{document}g0∈L2∩(KerL)⊥a,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _0^\infty \int a(p) |e^{tL} g_0(p)|^2 \, \textrm{d}p \, \textrm{d}t \lesssim \Vert g_0 \Vert _{L^2}^2. $$\end{document}∫0∞∫a(p)|etLg0(p)|2dpdt≲‖g0‖L22.

This corollary quantifies time decay for the solution, but it will be insufficient for our purposes. Indeed, the equation is ill-posed in weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 spaces, so that this topology cannot be used to control nonlinear terms.

In the next section, we aim to obtain pointwise decay, which will correct this shortcoming.

Pointwise decay for the linearized operator

In all that follows we assume that the chemical potential γ >0γ>0, that is we study the linearised operator around non-singular RJ equilibria.

In this section we investigate how the energy dissipation leads to a polynomially fast relaxation for the linearized semigroup, pointwise and away from the edges. To this purpose we explore how the edges of the domain, where the weight in the Poincaré Inequality of the previous section vanishes, interact with the bulk of the domain, where the weight is lower-bounded.

Energy decay in the bulk

We define that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle t \rangle = 10 + |t|, $$\end{document}⟨t⟩=10+|t|,as well as the following subintervals of [0,2π ][0,2π], corresponding to the edges and the bulk of the domain, respectively:5.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\mathcal {E}_{t,\alpha } = \{ p \in [0,2\pi ]: p< \langle t \rangle ^{-\alpha },\ p> 2\pi -\langle t \rangle ^{-\alpha } \} \text { and}\\&\mathcal {B}_{t,\alpha } = \{ p \in [0,2\pi ]: \langle t \rangle ^{-\alpha } \le p \le 2\pi - \langle t \rangle ^{-\alpha }\}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Et,α={p∈[0,2π]:p<⟨t⟩-α,p>2π-⟨t⟩-α}andBt,α={p∈[0,2π]:⟨t⟩-α≤p≤2π-⟨t⟩-α}.We now define the following functionals:5.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} m(t) = \int _{\mathcal {B}_{t,\alpha }} |g(t,p)|^2 d p, \quad n(t) = \int _{\mathcal {E}_{t,\alpha } }|g(t,p)|^2 d p, \quad q(t) = \sup _{p\ \in \ \mathcal {E}_{t,\alpha } } |g(t,p)|. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m(t)=∫Bt,α|g(t,p)|2dp,n(t)=∫Et,α|g(t,p)|2dp,q(t)=supp∈Et,α|g(t,p)|.

Lemma 6

Assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_0 \in L^2 \cap \big ( \operatorname {Ker}L\big )^\perp $$\end{document}g0∈L2∩(KerL)⊥, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha < \frac{3}{5}$$\end{document}α<35, and suppose that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ m(0)+n(0) \le 1 \qquad \text{ and } \qquad q(t) \le \langle t \rangle ^{e}\quad \text { for all } t \ge 0, $$\end{document}m(0)+n(0)≤1andq(t)≤⟨t⟩efor allt≥0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}e∈R. \frac{5\alpha }{3}}} \int _0^t \langle s \rangle ^{2e - \frac{8 \alpha }{3}} e^{c \langle s \rangle ^{1 - \frac{5\alpha }{3}}} \, \textrm{d}s \sim \langle t \rangle ^{2e -\alpha }, $$\end{document}e-c⟨t⟩1-5α3∫0t⟨s⟩2e-8α3ec⟨s⟩1-5α3ds∼⟨t⟩2e-α,where we use the identity5.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _1^T e^{t^a} t^b \, \textrm{d}t \sim \int _1^{T^a} e^s s^{\frac{b}{a} + \frac{1}{a} -1} \, \textrm{d}s \sim e^{T^a} T^{b-a+1}, \qquad \text{ if } T>2, a>0, b \in \mathbb {R} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫1Teatbdt∼∫1aessba+1a-1ds∼eaTb-a+1,ifT>2,a>0,b∈R(which is itself a consequence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _1^t e^s s^a \, \textrm{d}s \sim e^t t^a$$\end{document}∫1tessads∼etta, valid for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}a∈R). Thus we conclude that indeed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m(t) \lesssim \langle t \rangle ^{2e -\alpha }.$$\end{document}m(t)≲⟨t⟩2e-α.

Before we finish the proof we briefly verify the remaining claim that the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathcal {B}_{t,\alpha }}$$\end{document}GBt,α is invertible for t sufficiently large: This claim follows immediately using the fact that the set of invertible matrices is open subset of the space of 2× 22×2 matrices. Indeed we know that the full Gram matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathbb {T}}$$\end{document}GT is invertible since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _1, \phi _2$$\end{document}ϕ1,ϕ2 are linearly independent. Also, we write that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathcal {B}_{t,\alpha }} = G_{\mathbb {T}} - G_{\mathcal {E}_{t,\alpha }}, \quad \text { with }\ \left( G_{\mathcal {E}_{t,\alpha }}\right) _{i,j=1}^2:= \int _{\mathcal {E}_{t,\alpha }} \phi _i \phi _j, $$\end{document}GBt,α=GT-GEt,α,withGEt,αi,j=12:=∫Et,αϕiϕj,and we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \left( G_{\mathcal {E}_{t,\alpha }} \right) _{i,j}\right| \lesssim |\mathcal {E}_{t,\alpha }|\lesssim \langle t \rangle ^{-\alpha }, $$\end{document}GEt,αi,j≲|Et,α|≲⟨t⟩-α,since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _i, \phi _j$$\end{document}ϕi,ϕj are bounded. Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ G_{\mathcal {E}_{t,\alpha }} \rightarrow 0, \quad t\rightarrow \infty \quad \Rightarrow \quad G_{\mathcal {B}_{t,\alpha }} \rightarrow G_{\mathbb {T}}.$$\end{document}GEt,α→0,t→∞⇒GBt,α→GT.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathbb {T}}$$\end{document}GT is invertible and the set of invertible matrices is open, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_{\mathcal {B}_{t,\alpha }}$$\end{document}GBt,α is invertible for all sufficiently large t. □ □

A weak pointwise bound

We prove a very weak bound, which will be a stepping stone to start the proof of pointwise decay.

Lemma 7

Let g solve \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t g - L g =0$$\end{document}∂tg-Lg=0 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(t=0) =g_0 \in L^\infty $$\end{document}g(t=0)=g0∈L∞. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert g(t) \Vert _{L^\infty (\mathbb {T})} \lesssim \langle t \rangle ^{1+} \Vert g_0 \Vert _{L^\infty (\mathbb {T})}. $$\end{document}‖g(t)‖L∞(T)≲⟨t⟩1+‖g0‖L∞(T).

Proof

By the decomposition of L in Section 4, f solves the equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _t g = -ag - K_1 g + 2K_2 g. $$\end{document}∂tg=-ag-K1g+2K2g.We now want to bound the right-hand side in order to obtain an ODE satisfied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert g(t) \Vert _{L^\infty }$$\end{document}‖g(t)‖L∞. Since a \ge 0a≥0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \frac{d}{dt} \Vert g \Vert _{L^\infty } \lesssim \Vert K_1 g \Vert _{L^\infty } + \Vert K_2 g \Vert _{L^\infty }. $$\end{document}ddt‖g‖L∞≲‖K1g‖L∞+‖K2g‖L∞.The kernel of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_2$$\end{document}K2 is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \frac{\omega \omega _1 \omega _2 \omega _3 \mathfrak {f}_1 \mathfrak {f}_3}{F_+(p,p_2)} \lesssim \sqrt{\omega \omega _2} \omega _1 \omega _3$$\end{document}ωω1ω2ω3f1f3F+(p,p2)≲ωω2ω1ω3; hence it is uniformly bounded and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert K_2 g(t) \Vert _{L^\infty } \lesssim \Vert g(t) \Vert _{L^2} \lesssim \Vert g_0\Vert _{L^\infty }, $$\end{document}‖K2g(t)‖L∞≲‖g(t)‖L2≲‖g0‖L∞,since the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm of f is decreasing.

Turning to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1$$\end{document}K1, its kernel is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \frac{\omega \omega _1 \omega _2 \omega _3 \mathfrak {f}_2 \mathfrak {f}_3}{\sqrt{F_-(p,p_1)}} \textbf{1}_{F_-(p,p_1)>0}$$\end{document}ωω1ω2ω3f2f3F-(p,p1)1F-(p,p1)>0. that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Pointwise bounds

Lemma 8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha < \frac{3}{5}$$\end{document}α<35, and assume that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} ]$$\end{document}p∈[0,2π], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |g(t,p)| \lesssim |g_0(p)| + \omega ^{\frac{3}{2}-} \langle t \rangle ^{b+1+}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|g(t,p)|≲|g0(p)|+ω32-⟨t⟩b+1+.(ii)In the bulk: if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega > \langle t \rangle ^{-\alpha }$$\end{document}ω>⟨t⟩-α, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |g(t,p)| \lesssim \omega ^{-\frac{1}{6}} \langle t \rangle ^{b+} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|g(t,p)|≲ω-16⟨t⟩b+

(i)If b>-1b>-1, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in [0,2\pi ]$$\end{document}p∈[0,2π], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |g(t,p)| \lesssim |g_0(p)| + \omega ^{\frac{3}{2}-} \langle t \rangle ^{b+1+}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|g(t,p)|≲|g0(p)|+ω32-⟨t⟩b+1+.

If b>-1b>-1, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in [0,2\pi ]$$\end{document}p∈[0,2π], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |g(t,p)| \lesssim |g_0(p)| + \omega ^{\frac{3}{2}-} \langle t \rangle ^{b+1+}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|g(t,p)|≲|g0(p)|+ω32-⟨t⟩b+1+.

(ii)In the bulk: if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega > \langle t \rangle ^{-\alpha }$$\end{document}ω>⟨t⟩-α, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |g(t,p)| \lesssim \omega ^{-\frac{1}{6}} \langle t \rangle ^{b+} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|g(t,p)|≲ω-16⟨t⟩b+

In the bulk: if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega > \langle t \rangle ^{-\alpha }$$\end{document}ω>⟨t⟩-α, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |g(t,p)| \lesssim \omega ^{-\frac{1}{6}} \langle t \rangle ^{b+} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|g(t,p)|≲ω-16⟨t⟩b+

Proof

(i) We first split as above into the two integral kernels\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddt|g(p)|≲∫ωω1ω2ω3F+g(p2)dp2+∫ωω1ω2ω31F->0F-g(p1)dp1=I++I-.We start with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} close to 0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I_+ \lesssim \omega ^{\frac{3}{2}}\left( 1+ \sqrt{ \log \frac{2}{\omega }}\right) \Vert g\Vert _{L^2(\mathbb {T})}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}I+≲ω321+log2ω‖g‖L2(T).The case p close to 2 π 2π is symmetric, and if p is away from the edges, the integral \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} is easy since then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Iterative improvement

We will now apply iteratively Lemmas 6 and 8, having set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = \frac{3}{5} - \epsilon _0$$\end{document}α=35-ϵ0, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 <\epsilon _0 \ll 1$$\end{document}0<ϵ0≪1. Finally, applying Lemma 6 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e =- \frac{3}{5} \nu +$$\end{document}e=-35ν+ gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ m(t) \lesssim \langle t \rangle ^{- \frac{6 \nu }{5} - \frac{3}{5} +}, $$\end{document}m(t)≲⟨t⟩-6ν5-35+, and hence 5.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert g(t) \Vert _{L^2} \lesssim \langle t \rangle ^{- \frac{3 \nu }{5} - \frac{3}{10} +}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖g(t)‖L2≲⟨t⟩-3ν5-310+.

Finally, applying Lemma 6 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e =- \frac{3}{5} \nu +$$\end{document}e=-35ν+ gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ m(t) \lesssim \langle t \rangle ^{- \frac{6 \nu }{5} - \frac{3}{5} +}, $$\end{document}m(t)≲⟨t⟩-6ν5-35+, and hence 5.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert g(t) \Vert _{L^2} \lesssim \langle t \rangle ^{- \frac{3 \nu }{5} - \frac{3}{10} +}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖g(t)‖L2≲⟨t⟩-3ν5-310+.

Theorem 3

(Pointwise decay) Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_0 \in L^\infty $$\end{document}g0∈L∞, with a zero projection (in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {Ker} L$$\end{document}KerL. Then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ,\nu \in (\frac{1}{6}, \frac{1}{2})$$\end{document}μ,ν∈(16,12) and any δ >0δ>0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert \omega ^\mu e^{tL} g_0 \Vert _{L^\infty } \lesssim _\delta \langle t \rangle ^{- \frac{3}{5}(\mu + \nu )+ \delta } \Vert \omega ^{-\nu } g_0(p) \Vert _{L^\infty }. $$\end{document}‖ωμetLg0‖L∞≲δ⟨t⟩-35(μ+ν)+δ‖ω-νg0(p)‖L∞.
