Lemma 10

(A priori bound) There exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0 >0$$\end{document}C0>0 such that, for any T>0T>0, if g is a solution in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}([0,T],\omega ^{- \frac{1}{6}} L^\infty )$$\end{document}C([0,T],ω-16L∞), then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert g \Vert _{\mathcal {B}_T} \le C_0 \left[ \epsilon + \Vert g \Vert _{\mathcal {B}_T}^2 + \Vert g \Vert _{\mathcal {B}_T}^3 \right] , $$\end{document}‖g‖BT≤C0ϵ+‖g‖BT2+‖g‖BT3,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon := \Vert \omega ^{-\frac{1}{2}} g_0 \Vert _{L^\infty }$$\end{document}ϵ:=‖ω-12g0‖L∞ and where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _{\mathcal {B}_T}$$\end{document}‖·‖BT is defined in (6.3).

In the above lemma, we denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0 for the constant, just like in Lemma 9; it suffices to take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0 to be the largest of the two to avoid any confusion.

Lemma 11

(Bootstrap inequality) If x(t) is a continuous function on (0, T) such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ x(t) \le C_0 \left[ \epsilon + x(t)^2 + x(t)^3 \right] \qquad \text{ and } \qquad \lim _{t\rightarrow 0} x(t) \le C_0 \epsilon , $$\end{document}x(t)≤C0ϵ+x(t)2+x(t)3andlimt→0x(t)≤C0ϵ,then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ x(t) \le 2C_0 \epsilon \qquad \text{ for } \text{ any } \; t \in (0,T). $$\end{document}x(t)≤2C0ϵforanyt∈(0,T).

We now perform the bootstrap argument. Arguing by contradiction, let us assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0 < \infty $$\end{document}T0<∞. By Lemmas 10 and 11, we get that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert g \Vert _{\mathcal {B}_t} \le 2 C_o \epsilon $$\end{document}‖g‖Bt≤2Coϵ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t < T_0$$\end{document}t<T0. There this contradicts the blow up criterion in Lemma 9. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_0 = \infty $$\end{document}T0=∞. A new application of Lemmas 10 and 11 gives the desired result! □ □

Proof of Lemma 9

It is very similar to the proof of Theorem 1 above and Lemma 10 below, and will therefore be omitted. □ □

Proof of Lemma 10

Proof of Lemma 11

Consider the function\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ F: x \mapsto x - C_0 (\epsilon + x^2 + x^3). $$\end{document}F:x↦x-C0(ϵ+x2+x3).It is clear that F is negative on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,C_0 \epsilon + \eta ]$$\end{document}[0,C0ϵ+η], for some η >0η>0. It is also clear that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ F(2C_0 \epsilon ) = C_0 \epsilon - 4 C_0^3 \epsilon ^2 - 8 C_0^4 \epsilon ^3 = C_0 \epsilon (1 - 4 C_0^2 \epsilon - 8 C_0^3 \epsilon ^2) \ge C_0 \epsilon (1 - 4 C_0^2 \epsilon _0 - 8 C_0^3 \epsilon _0^2) \ge 0, $$\end{document}F(2C0ϵ)=C0ϵ-4C03ϵ2-8C04ϵ3=C0ϵ(1-4C02ϵ-8C03ϵ2)≥C0ϵ(1-4C02ϵ0-8C03ϵ02)≥0,provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _0$$\end{document}ϵ0 is chosen sufficiently small. The statement of the lemma follows by the intermediate value theorem. □ □

Mass and Energy of RJ spectra

In this section, we investigate the following question: given a mass and an energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M}_0, \mathcal {E}_0)$$\end{document}(M0,E0), is there a Rayleigh–Jeans spectrum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {f}$$\end{document}f such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(\mathfrak {f}) = \mathcal {M}_0, \mathcal {E}(\mathfrak {f}) = \mathcal {E}_0$$\end{document}M(f)=M0,E(f)=E0? It turns out that the ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_0/\mathcal {M}_0$$\end{document}E0/M0 decides whether such a RJ spectrum exists. We define the following functions:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathcal {M} (b, g)&= \int _{\mathbb {T}} \frac{\, \textrm{d}p}{b^{-1} \omega + g^{-1}}, \\ \mathcal {E} (b,g)&=\int _{\mathbb {T}} \frac{\omega \, \textrm{d}p}{b^{-1} \omega + g^{-1}}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(b,g)=∫Tdpb-1ω+g-1,E(b,g)=∫Tωdpb-1ω+g-1.Here 0<b, g < ∞ .0<b,g<∞. We readily see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< \mathcal {M}(b, g), \mathcal {E} (b,g) < \infty ,$$\end{document}0<M(b,g),E(b,g)<∞, and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} b^{-1} \mathcal {E} + g^{-1} \mathcal {M} = 2\pi . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}b-1E+g-1M=2π.Moreover, we have the following differential equation:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} b \partial _b \mathcal {M} + g \partial _g \mathcal {M} = \mathcal {M}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}b∂bM+g∂gM=M.We adopt polar coordinates to represent (b, g):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} b = r \cos \theta , \qquad g = r \sin \theta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}b=rcosθ,g=rsinθ.Here 0<r<∞ 0<r<∞ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< \theta < \pi /2$$\end{document}0<θ<π/2. By homogeneity,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {M} (b, g) = r \mathcal {M} (\cos \theta , \sin \theta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(b,g)=rM(cosθ,sinθ).Therefore, for a given pair of positive numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M}_0, \mathcal {E}_0)$$\end{document}(M0,E0), there exists (b, g) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M}_0, \mathcal {E}_0) = (\mathcal {M}(b,g), \mathcal {E}(b,g))$$\end{document}(M0,E0)=(M(b,g),E(b,g)) if and only if either of the following equations on θ θ are solvable:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathcal {E}_0}{\cos \theta } + \frac{\mathcal {M}_0}{\sin \theta } = 2\pi r = \frac{2\pi \mathcal {M}_0}{\mathcal {M}(\cos \theta , \sin \theta )}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E0cosθ+M0sinθ=2πr=2πM0M(cosθ,sinθ),or\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathcal {E}_0}{\mathcal {M}_0} = \frac{2\pi \cos \theta }{\mathcal {M}(\cos \theta , \sin \theta )} - To summarize, we have the following:

Proposition 7.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {M}_0, \mathcal {E}_0)$$\end{document}(M0,E0) be a pair of positive numbers. Then there exists a Rayleigh–Jeans spectrum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {f} = \frac{1}{\beta \omega + \gamma }$$\end{document}f=1βω+γ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< \beta , \gamma < \infty $$\end{document}0<β,γ<∞, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M} (\mathfrak {f} ) = \mathcal {M}_0$$\end{document}M(f)=M0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E} (\mathfrak {f} ) = \mathcal {E}_0$$\end{document}E(f)=E0 hold if and only if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ 0< \frac{\mathcal {E}_0}{\mathcal {M}_0} < \frac{2}{\pi }.$$\end{document}0<E0M0<2π.Moreover, if the latter is the case, such \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {f}$$\end{document}f is unique and determined by the following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \beta&= \left( r \cos \theta \right) ^{-1}, \qquad \gamma = \left( r \sin \theta \right) ^{-1}, \\ \theta&= \arctan \left[ \left( F^{-1} \left( \frac{\mathcal {E}_0}{\mathcal {M}_0}\right) \right) ^{-1} \right] , \qquad r = \frac{ \mathcal {M}_0}{\frac{\cos \theta }{2\pi } \int _{\mathbb {T}} \frac{\, \textrm{d}p}{\omega + \cot \theta }}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β=rcosθ-1,γ=rsinθ-1,θ=arctanF-1E0M0-1,r=M0cosθ2π∫Tdpω+cotθ.

Before finishing this section, let us comment on a related conjecture made in [18]. For the truncated Wave Kinetic Equation arising from NLS, the presence or absence of a finite-time blow-up is expected to depend on whether the ratio of mass to energy is sufficiently large. Thus, given our proposition above, we expect that initial data whose mass and energy do not correspond to a Rayleigh–Jeans distribution will form a condensate in finite time. Proving rigorously that these condensates are stationary solutions remains an interesting open problem.
